ROLE PLAYING IN TOLKIEN’S WORLD

INTRODUCTION

The Middle-earth Role Plaping system HERP) os designed to in-
troduce people to fantasy role playing (FRF) in J.R.R. Tolkien's
Middle-earth. [tis suitable for those who have never before played a
fantasy role playing game, as well.as more expenenced gamers who
art looking for a realistic, casy to play fantasy role playing (FRP)
svatem designed for relaclvely low lewel adventures (1st-10¢h levels).
li containa complete mules for handling mom of the common situa-
tions Chal ariae in FRP games, and a variety of tables thar add a great
deal of favor and detail io a game without decreasing playability.

J.B.R. Tolkien's Middle-earth provides an ideal setting for a fan-
tazy role playing game. It is arefectionof our world as we perceive it,
as well as a construction of mythology by a great and learned man.
Middle-earth is itself undying, living inthe minds of all who tread its
paths. Bach reader adds to ithisor herown vision. It is only macural,
then, to use this incredible foundation in a fantasy role playing oon-
text. In this way those close to Middle-carth can experience it in anew
way, filling the gaps and discovering the mysteries that have alway
comosrned them.

The sWidnle-enth Role Playing ween (MERP) i supported by a
variety of play aids. Such products can decrease the Gime and effort
required for the creation of an exciting game, and increase the
amount of realism and detasl ohtaimed during play. These play aids
include 1.0 E.'s Middle-earth Campaign Modules, which provide a
wellth of material concerning specific sections of Middle-carth.
LC. E.'s Middle-earth Adventure Modules provide specific areas and
sites for adventares that are complete ond ready to run.

In addition, 1.C.E.'s Rakenaster Systems provide an expanded
combat system, an expanded spell system, a more flexible character
development system, and guidelines for a campaign game or larger
scale game. These systems allow MERP to bo expanded to handle
higher level characters and to increase the variations and options
available to the Gamemaster and the players.

WHAT IS A FANTASY ROLE PLAYING GAME?

The easiest way to understand a role playing game isto think of it
asa work of ction such as anevel (ora play, ofa movie, etc.) Ina
novel Lhe aullvor determines the setting of the howe along with theac-
Lidns of all of the characters and thos the plot; however, in aroks play-
ing ganbe, the author (called the Gamemaster) only determines the
setting and aoe of the basic ebements of the plot. The actions of the
characters (and thus the plot) are determined during the game by the
game “players” and the Ganvemaster, Each of tee “players contrels
the actions of his “player character”, while the Gamemaster controls
the actions of all of the other characters (called non-player
characters). Thus each player assumes the role of (pale playa) his
character and the Gamemaster role plays the noa-player characters.
In other words a fantasy role playing game & a “living” navel where
interaction between ihe actors (characters) creas a constantly
evolving plot.

The Gamemaster also makes sure all of the characters perform ac-
lianas #hich are possible only within the framework of the setting that
he has developed (his “fantasy” world). This is where the “faniasy"
part and the “game” part coors inte the definition of a fantasy robe
playing game. A Gamenuster creates a setting which is wot imited by
the realities of our world, and thus the setting falls into the genre of
fiction known as “fantasy”. However, the Gamemaster usually uses a
set of “rules which define and control the physical realities of his
fantasy world. The we of these moles turns the process of creating ihe
role playing “nevel™ inte a game.

Thus, a fantasy pole playing (FRP) game is et in a fantasy world
whos reality ia mot defined by our workd, but instead is defined by a
set of game rules. The creation of the plot of a FRE game is an on-
going process which both the Giamemaster and players may affect,
but which neither controls. The plat is determined throagh the incer-
action of the characters with one another and the FRAP ganve’s setting.

Since fantasy role playing is a game ii should be interesting, ex-
citing. and challenging. Thus one of the main objectives of a PRP
game is for each player to take an the persona of his for her) player
character, reacting bo situations as the character would. This is the
biggest difference between FRP games and other panes such as chess.
or bridpe, A player's character is mM just a piece or 6 card; in a good
FRE game a player places himself in his character's position. The
Canvemaster uses detailed descriptions, drawings and maps to help
the players visualize the physteal settings and other characters. In ad-
ditkon cach player character should speak and react to the other
players a6 his character would. All of this creates an air of involve-
ment, excitement, and realism (in a fantasy setting of course).

The Gamemaster bas been described as the limited “author” of the
FRE game; actually, he functions as more than this. The
Canmvemaster not only describes everything which occurs in the game
as if inwere really happening to the player characters, but be also acts
asa referee or judge for situations in which the actions attempted by
characters must be resolved. The Gomemaster hos ta do a lot of
Preparaion before the game is actually played, He must develop the
setting and scenarioas for the play of the game, using the game rules,
and either material of bhi. own deslen or commercially available play
aids, Until the players actually encounter certain situations during
play, much material concerning the setting and the scenariois known
only to the Gamemagter. In addition, the Gamemuaster plays the roles
ef all of ihe characters and creatures wivo are mod player characters,
but nonetheless move and ace within the game setting.

The players cach develop and create a character using the rules of
the game and the help of the Gamemaster (for the character's
background and history], Each player character has certain
numerical ratings for his attributes, capabilities and skills. These
Tidings depend upon bow the Mayer develops his character using the
rules of the game, Ratings determine ow much of a chance the
character has of accomplishing certain actions. Many of the actions
that characters aliemipa during play have a chance of success and a
chance of failure. Therefore, cven though actions are inithated by the
Camemaster and the Players during the game, the success or failure
of these actions is determined by the rules, the characters’ ratings,
and the random factor of a roll of the dace.

Finally, a fantasy role playing game deals with adventure, magic,
action, danger, combat, treasure, heroes, villains, life and death. In
short, ina FRE game the players leave the real world behind for a
while, and enter a world where the fantastic is real and reality is
limited only by the imagination of the CGhanvemaster and the players
whemsel ves.




FRE in Middle-earth

ADVENTURING IN MIDDLE-EARTH

You can be an Elf, or a Hobbit, or a Man, or even an Ore with
Middle-earth Role Ploving (MERP). You can eat cakes in the Shire,
peruse books in Rivendell, or fight Dragons in the Withered Heath.
Rather than simply reading abt the servants of the Evil One, you
Can de somethiag to shep them. Hath the Black Riders.or spy.on the
men af Merdor — whatever you think it takes to accamplish pour
mnisian. Bul remember, dangers abound!

(EAP stirs Your imagination and Lranéiormna your dreams into ae-
Liu. By letling you lake part inthe ongoing drama, Chis gare bets you
step out of this world and stride boldly inte Middle-canth.

THE PLACE CALLED MIDDLE-EARTH

Middle-earth (or “Endor”") is the Middle Land, a continent in the
world called *Arda” ("The Realm”). Created by Era = the One = a5
a land for his Children, hhiddbe-carth isa magical place populated by
all sorts of special folk and strange beasts,

In Middle-earth, immortal Elwes, stalwart Dwarves, and simple
Hobbits live amidst a varied lot of mortal Men, struggling to survive
and prosper in the faceof myriad threats: Orciand Wargs, Trolls and
Giants, Wights and Wraiths, Dragons and Pell Beasts, and of course
the Lord of the Rings. Awesome incantations mold the landscape
and affect the weather, whik Rings of Power guard magical realms
— of torture soule and drive Free Folk ime Darkness

ee eae

+ aula sine

However, Middle-carth also reembles our Old Ward, for the
land of Enedlor shares moet of the woes and wonders gonected with
the younger “world of men.” Greed, avarice, prejodite, hate, and
fear all sand in the way of happiness. The ongoing strugele of Good
versus Evil and the tests of Fate dictate the fortunes of the enslaved
and free alike.

This. isa setting ideally suited to herote drama, It beckons brave, im-
quisitive, adventurous souls to undertake quests of fame or fortune,
of to endeavor to rescue unfortunate souls from the hemous grip of
the omnipresent Shadow

So, by Eru, go forth and try your hand at the pleasures amd pitfalls
of WER And may Eru be with you.

A SAMPLE FRP ADVENTURE

This section is an example of a fantasy role playing adventure, The
adventure is presented in the form of the dialogue between the
Players and the Gamemaster, with oder actiogs and information in
nalics. This sample does mot derail sone of the game mechanics and
rules used to reselve actions. Examples of these mechanisms ond
rules are presented in later sections.

The player characters are traveling on foot from Rivendell to Bree
along the Great Bast Read carrying a shipment of fine Dorwinien
wiih and some Chort (an herb). The characters are:

1) BHoah Aponar, a male Elven Mage io dresses strangely.

2) Ayitola Chinta Kari, a female Umli Animist with certain
fanatic tendencies,

4) Naug Zigildin, a Dwarven Warner of unrevegled pender
(bo many, female Dwarves appear to be male).

4) Droggo Nimblefingers Bracegriddle, a male Hobbit
Scout; rude people might call him a thief,

Any similarity between these characters and any persons (real oF

HOW TO PLAY: THE RULES AND GUIDELINES

1.0 INTRODUCTION

Most fantasy role playing games meed “males” (or guidelines) to
define and control ihe physical eabties of the world in which adven-
tures may lake place. easly, such rules are able to handle most of the
COMA BLU that ariecina PRE game without detracting from
the flaver ated detail of the plot of setiing of the game,

This specific set of rules handles the physical realities of people,
places and things in J.R.R. Tolkien's Middle-earth, Sections 2 and 3
handle character creation, development, and capabilities. Section 4
details most of the rules that affect the Gamemaster's iask of setting
Up and managing 4 world syatem (The Setting), Sections 5 and 4
futline how to resolve commonly occurring actions and activity dur-
Ing the Players” adventures. Finally, Section 7 provides a variety of
lists, tables, and record sheets that provide mechanisms for adding
flavor and detail to a game without decrensing playability.

The Gamemager and player should keep io oiimd that thes
“roles are really only guidelines to abd in the creation and ronning of
a fantasy role playing game. The Gamemaster shoukl feel free to
modify these cules to fit bis (or her) view or style of role playing, and
the players should realize that the Gamemaster is the final authority
Wher tt comes to rules interpretations or changes.

On the other hind, these rules and the (aamemaster's decisions
conceming them are the only gudce that the players hare bo their
characters’ aapabilities and the reality of Middle-<arth. Sa the
Qamemaser must be consistent and even-handed in hie decisions or
lhe players will lose the trust snd confideres in hina that ia neccessary
for a touly enjoyable and satisfying role playing game.

1.1 LEARNING TO USE MERP

The Gamvemaster should firat skim the rubles to pet an overall view
of the system, them he should read all of the rules thoroughly. [f a
section is mot understood imevediately, lt should be marked and
referred to again after all of the rules have been read. Frequent
examples are incloded to aid in absorbing the rules. The Gamemaster
need not memorice or fully analyec the significance of all of the mules
at first. The roles areorganined in. such a fashion that many situations
can be handled by referring to specific rules sections when they first
Arie.

The players should firat read Section | and 2, Then they should
generate a character by following the procedure and examples
outlined in detail im Section 3, referring to parts of Section 2 for
explanation of the variows aspects of a character, Finally they should
read Section fac thet they will anderiand what their options arein a
lactica) (usually oombar) situation. It id mee necessary for the players
to rend Sections 4 or 45, since they are concerned with how the
Gamermaster can handle the setting of the game, the plot elements,
anal other factors.

2.0 THE BASIC FACTORS DEFINING A CHARACTER

Tn a fantasy role Paying (FRE) game cach participant except for
the Gamemaster (OM) is a “player" and assumes the persona of one
of the individual characters; these characters are the player
characters (PC's). All of the other characters are cootralled by the
CGamemasier and are called non-player characters (MPC). Therearea
variety Of factors that control what cach player is capable of in a
fantasy role playing environment (e.g., Mental avrlbotes, pliysical
attributes, background, physical appearance, skills, bonuses, evc.).
Hew cach of thee factors is handled in the Middle-earth Rode
Playing ayatem is described and discuased in this section.

MERP is a role playing system designed to handle (simulate)
characters between Ist level and JOth level, Tt makes centain
restrictions and samplifications that are intended to make lewrmning
fantasy role playing quicker and easier, Peeple who master the entire
Ewatem and want more derail, realicm and higher kevel characters
should consider CLE: modulariced Rofentaster systems. Arn
Law dt Claw Low details non-spell combat, with expanded critical
Labbe, expanded armor ivypes, martial arts, and individual weapon
abtack tables. Spel! Lae details and exqaands spell lists amd spell
casting; it contains 112 spell lists, over SMM} spells, spells up to Mth
level, 15 types of spell-users, ond evil spell lists. Character Law £
Comnrign aw presents an expanded character development system;
with 19 professions amd amore detailed treatment of stats amd other
factors affecting a character

21 MENTAL AND PHYSICAL STATISTICS

The ase mental an physical atiribaies of a character are
represented by & stutistics culled sats: Strength (ST), Agility (AG),
Constitution (20), Intelligence (G0), Intuition (IT) and Presence
(PRE Each character has a numerical value on ascaeof 1] to 100 for
each of his stats. The wales of a stat indicates how it rates relative to
the same stat of other characters, The lower the value of a stat, the
weaker it is relative to the same stat of other characters. Relatively
high stats give bonuses (ane Section 2.12) which apply to attempes to
Accomplish cemain activities and actions.

2.11 DESCRIPTION OF STATS

STRENGTH IST): Not brode musculature, but the abilitytouse your
Muscles to your greatest! advantage. This stat affects a cheracter’s
cepabilitics in melee combat, carrying loads, and other activities
requiring strength. This is the prime stat fora Warrior.

AGILITY (AGE Manual dexterity, litheness, quickness, reaction
time, and speed are all reflected Oy ihis stat. This stat affects a
character's capabilities im defense, missile combat, movereent and
oilher maneuvers. This is the prime stat for a Seout,

CONSTITUTION (OO): The general health and well-being of the
character. This stat affects a character's capabilities to resist disease,
poison aed other hardships, as well as his ability to resist the effects
of pain, shock, and bleeding (concussion hits), Ths the pome stat
lor a Ruanger.

IN TELLIGEMCE (li): The reasoning, memory, and commen sense
of the character. This stat affects a character's capabilities in
welivities that require thoughe of insight; forexample, learning spell
lista (see Section 4.51), learning languages, reading runes, etc, This is
the prime stat tor a Mage,

PRESENCE (PR): A character's courage, bearing, self esteern,
charisma, and self-discipline, This stat allects the character's ap-
pearance, his ability to affect and control other characters, his seld-
control om critical tuations, and his abality bo draw upon bis imine
reserves. This is the prime stat for a Bard.

INTUITION ((T}): The relationship of the character to the all-
pervading force in mature (the Ainulindale, ihe Some which created
and shaped Arda) and things supernatural, including such
phenomenon as wisdom, luck, genius, and the favor of the Valar.
This stat affects the character's capacity to cast spells, use magic
items, perceive things (e.g., traps), and to perform a variety of other
activithes. This is the prime stat for an Animist.

2.12 STAT BONUSES

Certain bonuses and penalties may apply tea character's skills and
activities if his state are high enough or low enough. These stat
bonuses are given in Table AT-1, and may be altered by modifica-

2.5 BACKGROUND

Each race has a certain number of “background points" which
each character may apply to the 4 background options: special
abilities, special items, money, hobby skill ranks, stats, and
languages, Certain races may be restricted as to how many points
may be applied to each option (see Section 2.21). The Gamemaster
lay chooks 1O hn options in his own manner, Albermatively, be
may choose to allow ghe player to cheese from or roll on the
Background Options Table COT-2.

2.51 BACKGROUND OPTIONS

SPECIAL ABILITIES — Theae are certain extraordinary capabil-
ites that the character possesses, In many cases theese capabilities
distinguish the player character from the general populace and are
part of the reason that he has chosen to adventure rather than stay
home on the farm. The Ganmemaster may make up special abilities
designed fora character, Alcernatively, the plaver may choose from
or ration Table OGT-2. Por cach background point placed into this
background option, the character receives one special ability,

SPECIAL ITEMS — These are magic or unusual items that the
character has had handed down to him or which fate has placed in his
possession, The Gamenuister may create spacial items designed fora
Character or allow him to roll on Table COT-2. For each background
point placed into this background option, lve character receives one
apecial item. These items are keyed tothe spect fic Player character, or

2.6 EXPERIENCE AND ADVANCING LEVELS

Each character ina fantasy role-playing game has a “level” thas
represents how capable he is. Characters become more powerful and
skilled by advancing levels as they gain experience. Experience is
represented in play by experience points which the Gamemaster
awards to charactera for certain activities and achievements.
Marmally, 8 character starts play asa Lat level character and his level
increases 86 Ie acquires experience points from his adventures. A
character's bevel docs not necessarily imcrease cach time he gains
perience points; it increases when his experience point total reaches
certain points, as explained below.

2.63 ADVANCING A LEVEL

When a character advances. (goes wp) a level, he may develop skills
(increase his skill ranks) and update his bonuses. To develop a skill be
allocates development points to it (see Table O0iT-4) in order to in-
eréase the skill's rank. This process is identical to apprenticeship skill
developenent and is described in Secon 3.6. After all development
paints have been allocated to increasing skill ranks, the level bonuses
for professions (see Section 2.4) are wpdated, Finally, any skill
bonuses whose components have changed most be re-totaled, This
poocess is summanzed below:

1) Allocate development poimis to increase skill ranks,

2) Update skill rank bonuses.

3) [f necessary, roll for hits (body development) or a spell list.
4) Update level bonuses for the character's profession,

5) Retotal any skill banuses affected by 2) and 3h,

6) Increase your Power Point total (see 4.55),

2.7 KEEPING TRACK OF A CHARACTER

Ina fantasy rele plying game each player cual keep wack of che
Tacuors defining and affecting his character, while the Gamemaster
must keep track of the non-player characters. In the JWiddie-earnt
Role Playing aystem (WERP), cach player should keep track of his
one characher (his player character) an a Character Record Sheet.
The Character Record Sheet (RS-1) has places for all of the
important statistics amd factors which are important to a character.
The Gamemasier may want to we Character Record Sheets for
certain crucial non-player characters (NPC's), but in general he only
needs to keep track of their levels. This allows him to refer to the
Master Character Table ST -3 in order to obtain non-player character
bonuses required during play.

The rest of tis section discusses the Character Record Sheet am.
the different ype. of capabilithes and bonuses that the playersshoukl
keep track of.

2.71 THE CHARACTER RECORD SHEET

The Character Record Sheet has spece forall of the factors which
affect @ character's bomuses and capablities fe.g., skill bonuses,
languages known, spell lista known, Weight, weight, etc.). A player
should fill in all of the appropriate spaces (this process is descnbed
step by step in Section 3) and add the values in each of the rows,
putting the result in the “total” space at the end of the row, When this
process & completed the character is ready to play and has on his
Character Record Sheet all of the bonuses which can affect his
Actions.

Anyone wing MEAP may photocopy as many Character Record
Sheets as he wishes for wie in his game, however they may net be
copied for commercial usage. Players can use the Iack of the
Characer Record Sheet to keep track of the character's supyplies,
equipment and olber posetssioMs.

2.72 SKILL BONUSES

Skill bonuses are the numbers added to various rolls which deter-
mine the success of events and actions during Way. They represent
how capable a character is at certain activities. The basic classifica:
tions for skill bonuses are the Offensive Bonuses (OB), Defensive
Bonuwes (DB), Moving Maneuver Bonuses (MM), and Static
Maneuver Bonuses (S04). Each akill bongs has its classification in-
dicated on the (Character Record Shee.

Each skill bonus is the sum of several specific bonuses, each of
which has a columm under “Skill Bonases* on the Character Reoord
Sheet, The specific bonuses include the skill rank bonus (see Section
2.30), the stat bonus (see Section 2.12), the profession bonus (see Sec
tion 2,4), item bonuses (see Section4. 36), and any special bonuses. If
a bonus space containg an “X" then that specific type of bonus
doesn't apply be teat sell. Ufa space already hiasa number init, Uhven it
isa fined bonus and does no change.

Aga skill rank i increased by ome through development (sce Sec-
tiem 2.3), the increase is marked on the Character Record Sheet by
placing an "Xt" in the first open box on the row for that skill. Mowe.
ment and Aeaneuver skill ranks are limited bythe number of boxes on
the Character Record Sheet. Skills without bowes (i.¢., Base Spells,
Leadership, Defensive Banus, and all of the Resistance Rolls) never
have a skill rank. All other deils are oot limited as to how high the
skill ranks ain go; space comiderations allow only 15 boxes ta be in-
claded on the Character Record Sheet.

Offensive Bonuses: (0B) — Offensive Bonuses inclede all of the
weapon skills bonuses, the Directed Spells skill bonus, and the Base
Spells skill bonus. These bonuses are weed in combat te attack op
ponents with weapons and spells (see Section 6.6), The bomises are
added ta atack polls which are applied to the various aliack tales.

Defensive Bonus (1A) ond Defensive Equipment — A character's
Defensive Bonus is weed in combat as a subtraction from an oppo-
ments altack roll against the character, There is a section of the
Character Record Sheet with a apace for the type of armor being
wort, a space for the eatra bonus added tothe Defensive Bonus when
the character is wing ashield against an opponent, a space to indicate
& helm, a space to indicate arm greaves, and a space to indicate leg
greaves. Normally a shield can be used to increase # character's
Defensive Bonus against one for by 25.

Moving Maseurer Bonuses (MM) — Moving Maneuver Bonuses in-
clude all skill bonuses for skills involving a bot of movement: mave-
ment and general maneuver, climbing, riding, s#imming, and stalk.
ing. These bonuses.are added to maneuver rolls which are resolved on
the Moving Maneuver Table MT-1.

Siathe Maneaver Bonuses (84) — Static Maneuver Bouse include
all skill bonuses for skills not involving much movement: tracking,
hiding, picking locks, disarming traps, reading runes, wing ibems,
perception, amd leadership. These bonuses are added to maneuver
rolls which are resolved on the appropriate column of the Stacie
Maneuver Table AlT-2.

2/3 SPECIAL BONUSES (SP)

Several of the skill do not fall in the general pattern outlined
Above: Body Development, Ambash, and Stalk/Aide. The Body
Development Bones i: the oumber of hits which a character can take
befercs passing owl (sec Section 2.32), The Ambush Bonus is a
modification to-critical rolls resalting from a sneak attack on the rear
ofan opponent (see Section 2.32). The Stalk “Hide Bonus is a Moving
Maneuver Bonus for Stalking (avoiding detection during
mavement}, and a Static Maneuver Bonus for Hiding (avouing
devection without ovoving).

2.74 RESISTANCE ROLL BONUSES (RR)

Certain attacks occurring during play wall require a character tc
make a Resistance Rall to determine if or how an attack affects the
character. The types.of attacks are Essence spells, Channeling spells,
Poisons, and Diseases. The attack's level (caster level ifitisaspell, at-
tack lewd if a poison of disease) is cross-indexed with the target's
(defender's) level on the Resistance Table RRT to obtain a single
number. ln order to successfully resist the atiack, the Resistance Rall
Must bet greater than or squeal to this number (see the example in Sec-
Lion 85).

Resislance Roll Bonuses consist of aster bonus, any sem bonuses,
and race bonuses (place in one of the special spaces). The mace
bonuses can be obtalned from Table BT-3,

mid-thigh, and te the mabdle of the upper ann. (Characters may also
dlect to wear helmets, arm greaves, andor leg greaves. These pieces
af equipment allow the wearers to avold certain criticals, but they
also involve pemalines: helmets moody the Perception bonus by -§,
arm greaves modify the Offensive bonuses by -5, and kg greaves
modify Movement and blaneuver bonuses by -5. lf a character is
wearing any of this equipment, be should record it on his Character
Record Sheet (see Section 2.72). Armor, helmets, and greaves do nat
collet for encumbrance purposes (see below).

Helms and greaves may be metal or leather. Qace a plece of leather
equipment (helm or greaves) has procected the wearer from one
critical, it will be worthless until replaced or repaired, Metal equip-
ment has ne such limitations if properly kept wp and repaired. It is
assumed that a character performs such maintenance.

Shields may only be uwied with 1-Handed weapons (see Table
CST-1), and they require the fall use of cove arm. When using a shield
achanracter may increase bis Defensive bomus by +25 Agadir OG Op-
penment in front of himor te bis left. It is assumed theahield ls on the
left arm; the Gamemaster may wish bo allow characters to be left-
handed (10% chance) or ambadextrowel? %e chance).

Escumbrance — The movement and maneuver capabilities of a
character are affected by the weight of the material which he carries
on bis person (if they are worn, armor, clothes, helmets and greaves
already have their own penalties already figured in). Each character
shoukd total the weight of all of the equipment (other than material
that he is wearing: clothes, anmear, helevet, greaves, belt, cfc.) and
supplies that be carries (see Table ST-4 for weights), rounding down
io the nearest pound. Wf the total is over 15 pounds, a penalty may
result, A burden of 1 Ib, of lesswill result inno penaley, It is aaeumed
that smaller characters are rugged enough to bear this weight. Thisls
particularly trac for non-human races,

Table BT-5 gives the penalty for carrying exces’ werghi based upon
the weight of the character. To this penalty the character adds his
Strength bonus (if positive), giving his "Encumbrance Penalty". This
penalty isapplied to ALL movement and moving maneuvers. If run-
ning oF atlempling ta run, the eecumbrance penalty is applied before
the doubling effect for dowbled movement. An example of this con
be found in Section 3.72).

Stats — In the "STATS" section of his Character Record Sheet, the
characte should record his stata. The stats are pumbers between |
and (032 ghat represent the physical and mental capebilitles of the
character (see Section 2.1). These values are placed meat to thie stats in
the column marked “value*, Each stat value baa a gormal bonus
(from Table BT-1).and arace bonus (from Table BT=3); each of these
is placed in the appropriate column, The sum of these two bonuses is
placed in the “total” colummand is ased as.a sat bonus for calculating
skill bonuses,

Constant Information — Certain information concerning a
character is relauvely constant and umchanging. The Character
Record Sheet provides spaces for a character's name, race, height,
weight, hair, eves, demeanor, and profession. A spaceis also proved-
ed for any special properties the player might wish to record,

2.8 THE ROLE OF THE CHARACTER

There are certain factore which are not formalized in the Adiouile-
earth Role Playing system but which acill affect the rote of a
character, and in particular a player character. These are factors
which are not capabilities of or restrictions on the charter; mastead,
they are facets of his individual nature and temperament. ‘Thes:
factors are crucial in bringing & character to life in the tenms of the
game, and they make the character scem more realistic to the
character's player as well as the other players and the Gamemaster.
Three of the most important of these intangible factors are
Personality, Motivation and Alignment in the struggle between
Good and Evil in Middle-earth.

FERSONALITY — Many players may inject their own Personality
inte their character, which is very acceptable and enjoyable.
Hawever, sometimes it is exciting, stimulating and rewarding to
embody your character with a personality other thas your own; after
all, thisis FANTASY Role Playing. Whatever you decide to-do, it is
important 19 carefully consider your character's personality.

MOTIVATION — Another question that should be answered about
the character is: What is his motivation? What are his objectives in
the game? ]t may be to adventure and have @ good time. It may be to
amass aq much gold, wealth, and magic items as possible. Itmay be to
kill and fight. [t might be to defeat the evil minions of Sauron and
make the workd safe for Heamanity (or Hotbitity or Elfity of
Dhvarfity or Entity). Wo might be todefeat the naive dupes of the Valar
and bring the Rightful Ruler of Middle-earth to control of Middle-
earth (Sauron of even your character). Whatever your character's
moinauion. it really helps to flesh him out.

ALIGNMENT — You should determine your character's
philosophy, morals, and social tendencies: so we group these factors
under alignment. The easiest decisionis: isthe character good, evil or
neutral (neither good of evil, cut for yoursell)? In Middbe-carth
term the evil dudes are usually (but not always) dominated by
Sauron and are out to destroy the good guys (wieo eliher are passive
or are actively opposing Sauran).

The next question is: What are the morals and philosophy of the
character? — Does he believe that the end justifies the means’?
Saruman did and he was finally corrupted. — Does be respect rigid
laws? The Elves did mot: some evil beings do. — Is he fatalistic? A
pred person might feel that Sauron would win regardless.of what he
as an individual might do, so why bother resisting. — Is he a
hedonist? Good of evil, his prime concern would be to enjoy himsell,
All of these are interesting examples; there are many other options
that add dimension and complexity to a charécter.

3.0 CREATING AND MOLDING A CHARACTER

In a Fantasy Robe Playing game cach participant (player) except
for the Gamemaster (GM) assumes the persona of one of the
individual characters — his player character. Each player mausa first
“generate” his player character in order to determine the character's
attributes, background, physical appearance, skills, etc.3in short, he
must determine all of the various factors that control what the
character is capable of doing ina Fantasy Robe Playing environment.
To generate a character the player should perform each of the
following steps (as described in Sections 3.1 —3.8):

1) Decide in general what type of chanacter to play,
2) Roll the character's stats (sec Section 2, 1).

3) Choose a race for the character,

4) Develop the character's adoleesenes skills,

3) Choose a profession for the character

6) Chosse the character's background options.

7)  DBevebop the character’s apprenticestup skills.

#9 Qutfit the character,

9) Total the character’s penalties and bonuses.

10} Develop a persona for the character.

The Character Record Sheet is the key in generating a character.
This sheet is organized to aid you during the generating process. As
you follow each stepol the process, you should record your resultsam
this sheet. Use a pencil, since you will be changing and updating your
character rqgularly. The various parts of the Character Record Sheet
and whad mecds io go in them are discussed In Section 27, Some of
the generation stepa.are interchangeable — primarily sat generation,
race choice, and profession choke.

If the Gamemaster and players wish to avoid ihe process of
generating characters, they can use the Master Character Table §T-3,
which gives average bonuses for the various professions and levels.

Before beginning to generale a character, a player ahoukl make a
general decision aa to whal type of character he wants to have. This
decision should concern the character's profession, race, and his
physical and mental auribares.

3.2 CHOOSING RACE AND CULTURE

There are a variety of races (and cultures) that a player may choose
for his character in Middle-earth. These races are described in Sec-
then 2.21; they include certain general classifications that in fact
cover many races and culrores [@.p., Men include such races und
cultures as Haradrim, Dunlendings, Béernings, etc). The race chosen
affects a. character's bonuses for skills (see Section 2.31), his develop-
ment curing bis adolescence, his spectalalsilities, bis appearance, and
certain other factors (gee Section 2.22). Once the race (or culture) is
chosen the player récorda it on his Character Record Sheet. He
should aloo record any stat bonuses and Pesistance Boll bonuses due
to race (ae Table BT=3).

The Gamemaster may wish to restrict the players choices 1o certain
“acceptable” races. For example, a gamemester may not want to
allow player character Orcs or Trolls because members of those rames
are usually not free enough of external domination fevil masters,
such as Margi or Sauron) to be comtralled by a player. However, a
Camemaster running a game set in the 4th Age of Middle-carth (i.c.,
after tse destruction of Sauron and The Ring) might allow player
characérs io be Ores and Trolls.

In order to get a feeling of which races are wewally suitable for
player characters the Gamemaster should read the race descriptions
in Section 2.21. The Race Table CGT-3 gives races which we sugeest
might be suitable,

The Gamemadier may wish to require a random determination of
rece and culture based epon Table COT-3,

character's appearance, Factors affecting a character's appearance
Include height, weight, age, hair color, eye color, demeanor, etc.
These characteristics and their significance are described in Section
2.21 for each race (and culture). The Gamemaster must decide
whelkier to ahgn these characteristics or whether the ployers moy
chacde the characteristics within the limits Biven in the race deserip-
thos.

3.6 APPRENTICESHIP SKILL DEVELOPMENT

Atte background options anda profession have been chosen each
character most develop skills during bis apprenticeship. Based won
his profession, the character receives a certain number of Skill
development points in each of the skill categories as indicated in
Table GOT. This process is also used each time that a character's
level increases. The various skills and categories are described in.
Section 2.32.

‘The development Pants in @ given catepory May be used towards
increasing skill ranks (see Section 2.5) in that category, A skill rank
may be increased by one (and one only), by using one of these
development points, A skill rank may be incrensed by po by using a
total of three developmen ponds, A skill rank may not be increased
by mare than 2 ranks during the apprenticeship.

The caception to these rules are the Movement and Manewver
skills, A character may allocate to any of the Movement and
Maneuver skills as many of his development points that he bas
available in the Movement and Mlameuver category (and those he
transfers). Bach point increases the skill rank by one, regardices of
how many points are allocated to that skill.

TRANSFERRING DEVELOPMENT POINTS = Development
Points that have moat been wsed may be transfered to another
category. Lf the category thai the points are transferred to has an
initial development point total that is zero (e.2., Weapon Skills for a
Mage or Spell Lists for Warriors and Scouts), then thal cabegory
receives ane development point fer every four transferred. Lf the
category that the pods are transferred bo has an initial development
Point total that is oot zero, then thal category receives ome
develapment point for every bro transferred. Points may be
tranifered from several categorie. Unused pointe are loat., Poiats
from any catepory ar combination of categories may be used to
develop the Perception skill (ie., iransterred om a 1 te 1 basis).
SPELL LISTS = Each development point wsedd on aapell lst gives a
20% chance of “learning” (see Section 4.5) a aqeell lat, thus using 5
penis gives a 1% chance of learning a list. A gives character may
only rollonce divring appreativeship ta learn aapell lat. Ifa character
atbenmpels bo learn a list and fails, the development points carry over to
learning the Wit for the mext bevel (see Sectlon 2.63). These palate
should be recorded on the Character Reoord Sheet next bo the spell
list name. If eaough points are exyeended to give a 100%) chance of
learning Int, the character may expend more points to take a chance
of learning a second list (20% chance per pont). A character's
profession. (see Section 2,4) and race (see Section 2,21) determine
which spell lists he may leam,

4.72 CALCULATING THE ENCUMBRANCE PENALTY

After the character has been outfitted, the player should total the
weights of all of ihe character's equipment and supplies (other than
clothes and armor), rrunding down to the nearest pound. 1 the total
isover 15 pounds, a penalty may result. Table BT-5 pives the penalty
for carrying excess weight: this penalty applied to ALL movernent
anid moving maneuvers. If running of attempting to run {see Section
6.41) the encumbrance penalty is applied before the deabling effect
for doubled movement. This penalty should be placed in the “EN.
CUMBRANCE PENALTY" space on the Character Record Sheet,

3.73 TOTALING THE BONUSES.
Al this point the player must determine and total his bonuses for
skills and wher capabilities,

Skill Rank Bonuses: The number of skill ranks is totaled foreach skill
alid the bonus @ determined from Table BT! and recorded on the
Characler Record Sheet on the appropriate skill’s bonus Lime.

Sat Bomuses: For each stat, the stat bonus is obtained fram Table
BY-1, and the ractal stat bonus medification & obtained from Table
BT-3. Both are written in the appropriate space med to the 4tats on
the Character Record Shee, anal then the totals are written mexe te
them. These stat bonuses are also recorded on the bonus line of any
Skill to which they apply (see Section 2.7},

Professional Boooses: Each profession receives bonuses for cemain
Skills and skill categories (see Section 2,4). These bonuses are record-
ed on the appropriate bonus lines on the Character Record Sheet.

Tiem Bonuses: Certain magic tems provide bonuses bo certain akilla.
These bonuses should be recorded om the Character Record Sheet.

Special Bomuses: These bonuses can come from background options,
spells, armor penalties, etc. Some of these bonwecs are already on the
Character Record Sheet. Toral these bonuses for each kill and place
them in the appropriate spaces on the player Character Record Sheet.

Finally for each skill, all of the bosuses are somimed to obtain the
total bonus which is used in play.

1) A setting must be developed for action te lake place in.

y) A peneral plot or scenario must be outlined.

4) Deckions must be made concerning bow to handle certain
éléments of role playing feg.. adventures, magic, religion,
healing, polsomes, etc.).

Developing the componenis mentioned above requires a lot of

qumber of cammercial products which provide part or all of each of
these components. Adventure or campaign modules provide
settings, characters, and scenarios, while rulea ayatems such a5
MERP provide the guidelines for handling the various elements of
role playing. A Gamemaster should keep in mind that all gach play
aids are merely quidelines for his own creativity and imagination.
These modules ad rules are meant to bemodified and adapted to fit
the Giamemaster'’s concept of what he wants his “world system" to be

Mordor, and his minions),

4.1 THE SETTING

The setting fora game can wary according to the amount of time,
effort, of money a Ceamemaster is willing lospend. Fortunately fora
Onmemaster minning a game in Middle-canih, 2 eoriias Aneune
of the work has already been done — by J.R.R. Tolkeen. Further
detailed material is also available in the form of [.0C.E.% modules,
Playing aide such os maps, layouts of building and towns,
descriptions of various hazards, and provisions for ireasureé are all
necessary ingredients for a setting that is realistic and playable.

For the purpose of bringing beginning Gamemasters gradually
into fantasy role playing, MERP will amplify the process of
constructing and designing a setting for a game. The three basac types
of settings defined in. MERP are: civilized areas, the countryside, and
adventure sites. Each type of setting satisfies a different mead in a
fantasy role playing game, while the three taken together provide a
framework that will handle most situations that arise ina game,

4.441 CIVILIZED AREAS

Civilized areas in a fantasy role playing game provide a place for
player characters (and eather characters) to boy and sell things, io get
food and lodging, to get information on potential adventures, to heal
and recuperiie, etc, In other words a civilized area is a place that
Serves as a hase of operalions fora group of adventurers. These areas
aiso have potential for adventures such as attacks by thieves or
muggers, con jebs, tavern brawls, attempied polaonings, otc,
Suggestions for various activities of thistype and guidelines on how
to pun them are presented in Section 5.1.

A civilized area can be as simple as an iclated inn or castle (the
sample civilized area provided with MEAP is the inn at the Last
Bridge), or ae complex as a huge city with intricate internal politics
and adventures. In general, a civilized area setiing (see Part 111 fora
complete example} can be described by providing:

1) Mapa and laywuts.

2) A description of inhabitants and visitors,

3) A price list of available services, supplies and equipment.
4) Some dort of outline of available information and clues.
5) An authne of available healing and recuperation Facilities.
4) An auiline of posible hazards and adventeres,

7) A basic description of the area's current events and mood.

4.42 COUNTRYSIDE

The countryside in a fantagy role playing game includes all of the
gress of the world which are ner adventure sites (see Section 4. 13) or
avilized areas (see Section 4.11). The countryside is the setting in
which characters can travel, camp, explore, scout, hide, and evade
pursuing forces. Characters can also encounter creatures and other
charectersin the countryside, resulting in ambushes, fights, meeting,
sightings, etc, These activities and how to resolve them are detailed in
section §.2 Basically, the coumtryside is the general setting in which
the civilized areas and adyvennure sites are placed,

The countryside that a Gamemaster uses can be represented by a
simple pencil sketch of asmall area with the primary lerrain features,
the localiogs of the civilized areas, and the locations of dhe adventure
sites all marked. Alternatively, the countryside can include all of
Middle-earth in a very geoeral form (from the maps in J.R.R.
Tolkien's works and LC.E.'s Middle-carth continent mop), along
with very detailed map: and descriptions of the epecific region in
which the game is taking Mace. The map and sanyple terra Lrydaull
provided in MEAP are cood examples of a detailed countrys. In
peneral, an adequate countryside (sec Part (11 for a complete ecam-
fle} can be descobed by providing:

1) Asketch map of the overall world.

2) Detailed maps of the specific area in which the adventures occur,

3) The location of civilized areas and adventure wtes.

4) Sketches of typical terrain that might be encountered.

%) A description of normal creatures and inhabitants.

4) A description of characters and creatures whe might pass
through.

7) A list of possible encounters and hazards (including weather).

4.13 ADVENTURE SITES

Adventure sites are specific places which provideopportunities for
characters.to sneak around, explore, fight, loot, and all of the other
things that contribute tothe central objective of fantasy robe playing
— adventure. Section 4,4 describes some suggested types of adven-
Lures. Adventure sites usally have several conics altribuves: they
have a physical layout (a castle, a ruin, a cave complex, a temple,
elc.), they have some sort of danger (guards, hostile inbabvtante,
traps, etc.), amd they have some sort of objective or goal (treasure,
rescuing captives, wiping Out evil creatures, etc). These beatures are
described! in the following sections, Activitics in adweniure sabes are
resolved as actions ina tactical eoviranment (see Section 6.0).

The range of the complexity of an adventure site varies from a
simple cave (for as example see the Troll cave in Part 11), toa roined
castle (for an example see the castle in Part 11), to the complexicy of
ruined cities and hollowed aut mountains. In general, an adventure
sate Gee Part Ll for two complete examples) can be described by pro-
wicling:

I} Why the place was designed and built originally.
2) What (if anything) has happened to make the place an adventure
site.

3} What the place currently is used for.

4) Who or what lives there, or visits frequently.

5) The layout and its immediate surrounding area.
4) The hazards (traps, guards and inhabitants),

" T) “Amy treasure or goal.

Physical Layouts — To mun characters in aa adventure site the
Gamemaster must have at least asketch of the site's physical layout.
This can be a simple sketch of the moms, door, walls, corridors,
towers, etc, and their relationship io one another. The layouts in Part
D1 provide an example of how toapproach designing adventure sites.

Hazards — Inorder for an adventure site to provide exciting action,
there must be hazards. This may take the form of guards, traps, and
hostile inhabitants tike Men, Ores, Trolls, and other creatures (see
Section 4.7). The hasands may inclode natoreal obstacles like sheer
walls, chasms, collapsing walls, ete. Their location should be marked
on the adventure site's physical layouts. The adventure aites In Part
HL provide cxamples of some of these hazards.

Goals and Objectives — Characters have to have some reason co visit
an adventure site. This can take the form of a missin, such as reseu-
ing a prisoner, scouting out the adventure site, assassinating some-
one, stealing axmething, and 2 variety of things limited only by the
imagination. Many times it may just be a desire to fight, loot, and
pillage — the key word being LOOT.

Treasore — Table ST-6 provides.alistof some of thetypes of treasure
that might be found lin an adventure site: money, gems, Weapons,
anmor, magic items, spells om scrolls, spells in potions, and chwes to
other objectives (maps, books, inscriptions, etc.) Treaaure should
nit be reetrloted to this table: magic items and spells are only limited
by the imagination. Very powerful items are called artifacts and
usually have adetailed history, purpose and alignment (i.c., good or
evil), Magic items are discussed in more detail in Section 4.36. A
Camemasier must be careful when he sets up the treasure af an
adventure site. Too mech money or too powerful lems can un-
balance a game, while players can lose interest in agame wlth too lis-
tle money of items.

4.25 ASSOCIATES OF THE PLAYER CHARACTERS

Often a Gamemaster and players will want to have mon-player
characters that associate of adveniure with the group of player
characters. This is often necessary when there are not enough player
characters ta make a group large enough to handle a particular
adventure. In addition, it is often helpful to have allies or a powerful
friend to help balance overly powerful foes, In Tolkien's novel, The
Hobo, Gandalf fulfilled this role for the group of Dwarves and
Bilbo during their adventures. The “plot” (see Section 4.3) of the
Gamemasters world will heavily influence how auch non-player
characters are iatroduced and used.

Hiring Non-player Characters — Player characters may wish to hire
non-player characters to adventure with them. A Gamemasber must
decide what kiad aod how many characters are available ag a given
time and civilized area (see Section $.1 for anexamplc). The number
of availablecharacters should be limited in the smaller civilized areas.
ln Middle-earth Warriors and Scouts should be the most available,
with Bards and Rangers being less common, Animisis being rare, and
Mages. being even rarer.

To hire a non-player character, the Gamemaster should require a
roll on the Influence and Interaction Column of the Static Maneuver
Table (MT-2). For a given civilized area the Gamemaster should rate:
the difficulty of hiring non-player characters whe are looking to po
adventuring. The following ratings for difficulty are based upon the
offer made by the character actempting to hire. They might be ap-
plicable for the sample adventure in Section 8.0 (it should vary based
upon how many players there arch. AL “share” is the total money value
of non-magic treasure (gp, sp, bp, <p, tp, and gems; see Section 4.8)
obtained during the adventure divided by the number of non-hired
members.

Difficulty Offer Made

After being hired, the Gamemaaster atill controls the hired
charwcter, but the hirer should decide bis actions in non-crucial or
non-dangerous situations. Unless the hirer makes a successful in-
fuence.or interaction poll, the Gamemaster should make a “reaction”
roll for the hired character in dangercus of distasteful situations to
determine his actions. A relatively low roll would indicate an unwill-
ingness Lo enter or deal with the situation, while a relatively high rel
weruld indicate willingness.

Allies = Certain non-player characters may be allies of the player
charactersand ald themif they happen to be on ile same mission ar in
the same area. A good example of this is the mecting between Frodo
and Sam with Faramir and the Rangers of Ithilben in the forests of
North Ihilien. Frodo and Faramir were “allies”, and Foramir gave
Frodo advice, aid, amd a place to rest. The Gamemmaster may wish to
structure his game eo that the player characters have allies to turn to
for healing, information and help.

Guardians — While hired characters and allies are more passive ox-
amples of the type of help a Gamemaster may give to player
characters, “guardians” are more active forms of aid. Guardians are
non-player characters who either show up when needed or when sum-
moned by the player characters. For example, Tom Bombadil was a
guardian for Frodo and his friends for a linte while after they left the
Old Forest. When they were about tobe destroyed by Wights, Frodo
summoned Tom Bombadil by reciting a verse. We suggest that guar-
dians be limited by distance, the number of times that they may be
called, or the severity of the danger faced by the player characters.
Guardians can Vary in thelr power and effectiveness,

4.3 THE PLOT

The plot for a fantasy role playing game consists of the general
politics of the world, the activities and objectives of the non-player
characters, the goals and incentives for the player characters, and the
interactions between all of hese elements, A plot can be as simple as a
rubbed tower inhabited with bandits anc iheir treasure (the player
characters want the loot). On the other hand, a plot can be as
complex as the plot in The Lord of she Rings, We suggest a simple
plot for beginners. Part (11 provides such a plot with a castle holding
the posebility for treasure and adventure,
4.31 GENERAL POLITICS

Sketching out the general politics fora game helps the Gamemaster
make decisions concerning the actions of non-player characters, I
abo helps him make the player characters’ backgrounds come to life.
It provides a framework for the general course of events in the
regions that the player characters travel through. Forexample, a war
of armed raid may be taking place in an area where the player
characters are adwenluring.

The World Politics — The politics of Middle-earth during the Second
and Third Ages are fairly simple: Sauron and his forces are crying to
take over the known world through direct conflict (war) or throweh
subversion and corruption of the Free Peoples, The Free Peoples are
irying {in a disunified fashion) to resist Sauron's expansion and
hopefully to defeat him. In between we have neutral forces being
influenced by the tem extremes. Through all this Many separate
kingdoms and cultures have their own internal polilics and
objectives. The specific pollical situation (How powerful is Sauron?
What is be doing? What are the Free Peoples doing? ete.) should be
based upon the time in which the game is being run. Gartemasters
may gather seeded material along these lines from Tolkiens's works
and 1.0C.E."s Campaign and Adventure Guidebook to Middle-earth.

The Fourth Age (ie., after the destruction of The Ring and
Sauron) allows fora greater flexibility in determining the politics of
Middle-earth. The Gamemasier may develop any sort of political
sitwation that he wishes, restricted only by the history of the earlier
ages, During this period, we suggest the main political organizations
beempire, kingdoms, guilds, religious groups, andthe like. Much of
the conflict in Middle-earth can result because of conflicts between
such groups, Truly evil (a opposed to self-interested) organizations
could be included in the form of the remnants of Sauran’s forces,
Pirates, bandits, etc.

Regional Politics — The politics of the specific region being adven-
tured in should be more detailed than the world politics, Queeions
that should be answered include: Are Sauron’s forces active in the
area? 1s Sauron corrupting the local rulers? Are the local rulers
already evil? Are the local rulers im conilic’? Where are the head-
quarters of the opposing sides? How do both sides treat characters
DOC involved in the conflict? [ni short, any activiiesof the locals that
tight affect the player characters should be outlined,

4.32 NPC OBJECTIVES AND PLANS

‘The objectives and plana of any significant non-player characters
should be noted. The objective of a farmer would be to bring in his
eropa; shopkeepers and traders want to sell high amd buy low; kecal
officiaks want to collect taxes and keep things peaceful; etc. More
wnuiwel characters may have personal objectives that conflict or
coincide with the player characters. Such objectives should be
carefully thought owt.

4.33 PC GOALS AND INCENTIVES

The player characters should set their own goals (sometimes with
help from their Gamemaster through background details) However,
to maintain 2 exciting game, the Gamemaster should supply incen-
tives. A good adventure should have the danger and rewards balanc-
ed. Obtaining treasure, experience points, crucial information, and
the like should involve risk, difficulty, and good play. This is
SOMETHIN Lhal reypuires experience and a good Ciumemaster, 50 it
may take several adventures to master.

4.41 SCENARIOS

These scenarios are adventures that can be keyed to a epecific
region or adventure site. The Gamemaster may wish to make in-
formation concerning several of these available to the player char-
acters and hope that they pick one, Alternatively, he may wish to
require that a scenario be chosen because it is the only one that the
Gaumemaster has available.

Missioms — Missions are often adventures in which a non-player
character sends the player character to. an adventure site or region to
accomplish a certain goal, The non-player character may or may not
accompany (he group, and he may oF may no give therm all of the in-
formation that he has on the mission, Often the oon-player character
haa ulterior motives and is willing to pay for the services of the player
characiers.

Sometimes the player characters have missions of their own
chooding. Examples of this might be: rescuing a captured growp
member, rescuing a friend or relative, searching for herbs to heal an
injured group member, or searching for a specific magic item.
Basically any goal of the player characters can be used to initiate a
missicen.

Scouting — Scouting missions do not necessarily take place in
adventure sites; they may consist of general investigation of the
couniryaide. In a scouting misshon the main goul is information,
either for a non-player character or for the player characters them-
aclves. Such information coukl Laver lea! to a specific mission.
Scouting minions often Iead to combat in the form of emcounvers
with hostile guards or patrols.

Balding — Raiding is an adventure designed to attack, fight and loot.
It docsat have io be keyed to a specific advencare site. Player
characters are out for any appropriate fight with a suitable reward
{treasure and experience points).

Lowting Attack — A looting attack is basically the same as raiding,
except ihat the player characters are attacking a specific adventure
Bib,

Toumaments — Tournaments are aged competitions in fighting,
archery, tiding, and other combat skillz, These are usually held in
civilized areas during fairs and holidays. Cash prizes are uxwally of-
fered, and on rare occasions magic items are awarded. Usually the
Combatants are not Lying bo injure or kill one another, but secidests
do happen. During tournaments, unless a participant is crying to kill
someone, all criticals inflicted are reduced to “A's” regardless of the
result fram the attack table.

Duels — Duels are also very interesting, Player characters or non-
Player characters often feel that they have a basic disagreement that
can ne be settled by talking. [f neither party feels inclined to run
away and leave town, a duel might result. This can range from a duel
in which the winter ia the first io draw blood using only "A" criticala
(see above), 10 a duel to the death.

Middle-earth had unique tes to the Undying Lands which created

When constructing the setting for a fantasy role playing game

4.51 ESSENCE

The Essence realm utilizes the power thet exists in everyone and
everything of and in Asda, and in particular Micke-canth, Essence in
Middle-earth has its source in The Song (The Ainalindak) which
created Arda and the order of things. A character who costs spells of
the Essence taps this power, oeelds it, ad diverts it into his spells.
Moat of the traly powerful Essence spells deal with forces elemental
in nature: fire, earth, water, wind, light, cold, etc. The more inert
material that is on the person of someone casting Essence spells, the
avore difficult it becomes to manipulate the Essence. Thus, when
casting or using an Essence spell WO armor, preavesor helmet mary be
WwOTE.

4.52 CHANNELING

The Valar are the offspring of Bru (God) who came to Arda to
fulfill The Song. The Channeling realm utilizes the power of the
Vala as channeled through characters casting spells. Sloe the ¥alar
are no. part of The Song (they helped to develop part of The Song,
and Morgoth conupted pant of it), Channeling is independent of the
Essence. A character caeting Channeling spells draws directly fram
the power of ane or more of the Valar, even though the does mot
*usually” require the conackuis cooperation of the Valar involved.
Thus spells of this realm do mot necessarily reflect the nature of the
Valar as long a3 the spells are relatively subtle and low level (.g.,
healing, detection, ete). Mare powerful and higher level spells mot
included in MEAP might require active consent of the Valar (the
Vilar inchide Morgoth, s0 evil spells may be cast ifthe power cores
from him). Metal interferes with drawing power from the Valar, so
ne metal armer, metal heloets, or metal greaves may be worn when
fasting a Channeling spell.

4.53 SPELL LISTS

Ac spell list is an ordering of spells based upon the correlation of

(ent Secthoen 4.54) am entire spell st of related spells and is limited

The spell lists are in Section 7.1. Each spell list fallsinto one of six
groups: Essence Open Lisis, Mage Lists, Bard Lists, Channeling
Open Lists, Animist Lists, and Ranger Lists. These classifications
determine which spell lists are learnable by which professions (see

its level, Each spell has an area of effect, a duration (how long do the

elie ul falls into. Bach of these factors is described in Section 7.1.

4.64 LEARNING SPELL LISTS

Ac character may “learn” spell lists during his adotescence skill

teen 2.63). Depending upon hit profession (ase Secton 2.4) and race

Normally, once a character learns a spell list he may cast any spell
ort that fsd that is his level or lower. Certain professions may only cast
spell up to a certain level (see Section 2.4).

4.55 CASTING SPELLS

A character may cast a spell from a list that he knows if the spell
lewel is less then or equal to his own level ANID be bas enough power
points, Certain professions.are limited as to the highest level spell that
they may cast (pee Section 2.4). Whenever a spell is cast an attack roll
muel be made and resolved on the appropriate attack table (see Sec-

and resolved on the Base Attack Table (AT-9) to determine if the
spell faik.
Power Points — Each character has a certain number of power
poiocs available to use for casting spells. In onder to cast a spell, a
nomber of power points equal to the spell's level must be paed,

Each character hada numberof power points based upon his level
and ome of his state (Intelligence for Essence spells and Intuition for
Channeling spells). The number of power points that a character has
is obtained by checking Table ET-1 for the appropriate stat and
multiplying the given power points by the character's level. Once
these points are used casting spells, they may be regained by resting
for § hours.
Time Required to Cast Spells — 1; takes one round of action to casta
spell (see Section 6.3). However, up to 4 rounds can be spent prepar-
ing a spell. The more rounds spent preparing a spell, the more effec.
tive the spell. The attack roll forthe spell is modified according to the
number of rounds spent preparing the spell. Instantancous spells
(marked with an *")are unaffected by these proparation modifica-
tions, and thus do not incur a penalty if cast without preparation.

Religious Worship

Religious practices vary with locale, but those found in morth-
western Middle-carth are generally informal, Personal meditation
and infrequent communal celebrations dominate the ritual life of
the region. Elsewhere in Middle-earth acrict or formal relighows
norms are more widespread.

4.7 INJURY, DEATH, AND HEALING

Daring the course of a game characters will likely receive damage
in the form of ingury or death tsorry, but that's life in Middle-earth).
There are two mapor factors in damage to a character: injuries to the
hely of the character and death (the depariure of the soul from the
body). Injuries to the body can lead to “death”, Each of these two
types of damage requires separate types of healing,

4.72 DEATH
Death (departure of the soul) occurs in one of four ways:

1) Immediate Death — An injury occurs which causes the character
to dhe immediately.

2) Excessive Hits — Ifa character receives more hits than the sum of
his Body Development bit total (see Section 2.32) and his Constitu-
tian stat, the soul will depart after & rounds. If someone heals
enough of these klts (before the 4 rounds are up) to drop the total
under the sun mentioned above, teen death will mot occur due to-ex-
oeisive hits.

3) Death After a Number of Rounds — Lf an injury occurs which
eperminves thal the character will die after a number of rounds duc
bo the injury, then dhe soul will depart after that many rounds have
passed. If the injury leading to death healed before the rounds
have posse, the character will not die due to thos injury.

4) Special — Certain special spell or occurrences may bead clirectly
to death,

4.73 NATURAL HEALING AND FIRST AID

In a fantasy role playing game healing is very important. It deter-
mines how often characters die asd how much time and money are
required for recovery from injury. Injuries heal noturally and may
be temporarily patched as follows:

Hits — One every hour if resting, ome every 3 hours if not resting.

Hits per round — A bandage and compress will stop 1, 2.07 3 hits per
round for reduce a hits per round “wound by 3), a0 long os the
character does not de more than walk during the mext 8 hours (the
hits per round earl again if he dows mone than walk). A tourniquet
will dep dite WO hits per round from a limb wound, but until the
wound i: healed the tourniquet must be loosened once every 24
rounds and 3 bie per round must be taken for S rownds tor the limb
wall be iat).

Sohiractions to Activily — These are eliminated and activity is
restored to normal when the Injury causing them bb healed.

An estimate of the average natural recover time for specific in-
juries can be obtained from Table UHT-2. These tleees are based
upon @ period of rest and recuperation, and proper treatment (e.g...
setting of broken bones, bandaging of wounds, proper Muiriion,
etc.). The Gamemaster sould base the severity upon the description
af the damage.

4.74 HEALING WITH SPELLS

Aunimists have certain spell lists that allow them to heal injeries,
preserve the body, and prevent the soul from leaving the body. The
spells that heal injuries are specifically defied in the spell list
descriptions (Section 7.1).

The Preservation spell on the Direct Channel list (see Section
7.15) halts decay of the body so that the deterioration of stats
described above in Section 4.7 will mot occur, of will stop if it has
started. The Lifekeeping spell on the same list will prevent the soul
from leaving a body thar would otherwise die without the injuries
being healed as described above in Section 4.7. Once a soul haslefta
body, Lifekeepine will NOT bring it back: Lifegiving would be re-
quired. Lifegiving is at least a 12th level spell, and will vary depen-
ding upon the level of the character casting it as given in Table
JHT-3. This assumes that an Animist of that level is available, Each
Lifegiving spell (based upon bevel) may only be attempted once fora
“dead character, but the me time that character “dies* each spell
May be used again.

5.0 ACTION IN THE STRATEGIC ENVIRONMENT

The strategic environoent conslats of the civilized areas and the
countryside (see Section 4.1). Compared to tactical action (see Sec-
tion 6.0), which usually takes place in adventure sites, action that
takes place ina strategic environment is usually less dangerous and
requires less Gime to resolve and describe. Sach action can often be
resolved im very hile real tlave (aa opposed to time within the
framework of the panel.

5.1 ACTIVITY IN CIVILIZED AREAS

Activity in civilized areas can be as simple as rewurnlng after cach
adventure, healing, re-equipping, and heading back oul lo adven-
ture agoin, However, it is often very intereding lo make a Vall toa
civilized ores more involved than that. A civilized place can be a
saurce of information, intrigue, recreation, and dangerous en-
counters
6.11 BUYING AND SELLING

Each character can wiually handle his own buying amd gelling of
normal goods (supplies and items) on his own. This process and a
monetary system is described in Sections 4,56 and 48, amd prices are
available in Tables ST-4, ST-5, and S7-7. However, certain unusual
parts of the process may involve trading and dickering, and the
Gamemaster should become involved as the person that the
character is trying to trade with. The Gamemuaster must also deter-
mine if sellers or buyers exist for unusual or spectal tems or herbs.
Normally this is part of the description of the civilized area.
6.12 FOOD AND LODGING

Each civilized area should have facilities for food anal loxbging,
even if it is only swill or table scraps and a barn. Table ST=4 gives
some suggested prices. for food and lodging at a normal inn in a
wen. The Gamemaster may make a roll for each inn for lodging
desined) visited to determine the amount of activity for that day.
Very high means that there is no room for the characters; very low
means that they might be able to get cheaper rates,

6.13 GATHERING INFORMATION

Often a civiliced arca is the place to pick up information about
adventure sites, missions, and other scenarios deacribed in Section
4.41, Information on missions that non-player characters wand perr-
formed should be made available at inna and public places, citer
through werd of mouth or direct contac! with the peron who wants

like wanted posters.

Rumors and choes about local occurrences, creatures, and adven-
ture sites shoukd be available from local people in bars and inns.
Often i will help to get the decals drunk of ina good mood bo pet bots
of information. Such information should be in the form of pmo,
legends, tall tales, and outright lics.

Reliadic information like maps and deacnptions (from characters
who really know what i going on) should be available fora price.
This type of information can be bartered for like any other oom-
modity (see Section 4_F}.

The Gamemaster may wish to allow characters to make
“background legend rolls” on a given topic, in order td remember
tabes, legends or other information from their background or past
history. Bards are professionally adept at this and should be given
an extra bonus of +45 per level, because tt is assumed thal they con-
tinwally increase and update their knowledge base. The Gamemaster

cvilined area and ihe situaiion of the player characters.

their possessions, an attempt to cell the pliyer characters fake goods
{or information), an attack by outside forces on the civilized area,
cic, The possibilities are endlest, and often lead to an adventure
which cam fill out an entire ganve session.

527 CAMP SITES FOR RESTING AND SLEEPING

While traveling it is assumed that the group resis for about 15

A group can travel 8 to 12 hours a day, and it is assumed they make

heowrs it is assumed that they have found a pood, defensible camp
aie. Otherwise, the Camenmaater dhould make an open-ended roll ro
help him decide the quality of the camp site found. A lew roll in-
dicate: a poor camp sie and possible dangerous surroundings. A
high roll indicates a gocdl camp site.

Monmally, the player characters will sei a watch in shifts (usually
3, 4 or Sahilts), bot the Gamemaster should aot suggest it or remind

5.23 EVASION AND HIDING

Sometimes a group will find itself in the position of being pursued
through the countryside by hostile forces. This may take the form of
being chased by another group (direct pursuit), or it might take the
form of a general hue and ery (1e., the whole countryside has been
alerted and is looking for the growp).

A general hue and ery is handled by rolling for encounters (poe
Section §.24) once an hour instead of one every 4 ours, As for
divect pursuit, most of the time the purswed group will want ta.avodd
capture or conflict (evasion and hiding). Other times they will want
toumbbush the pursuing group (handle as a normal combat). Direct
Pursuit i¢ handled once ewery hour as follows:

1) Determine the distance separating the pursued group and
the pursuing group.

2) The pursuing group must make a tracking maneaver,
modified by adding one pursuing character's Tracking
bonus and subtracting one purswed character's tracking
roll. LE the pursued group is unaware of the pursuit and
does not try to cover their tracks, only the Tracking
bonus of one pursuing character and + 40 are added to
the ral.

3) Uf the tracking manéuver i a “sucesss” or an “abselate
succes” (from Table MT-2), the distance separating the
two groupe is modified by the difference in their move-
ment rates. Each “near success” before a “success” in-
creases the distance separating the groups by 200" (moat
af the time this can be ienored).

4) If the tracking maneuver is a “partial success", the
datance separating the groups is increased by the move-
ment rate of the purswed groupe.

§) The other tracking maneuver results are self-explanatory
failures.

If the pursuers sight the pursued group, action should be resolved
as a tactical situation (jee Section 6.0). In rain (or mow) if the
distance separating the two groups exceeds the distance thal the pur-
SETS Can cover in one hour, the parsait is enced (less for very heavy
rain, more for light rain}.

alty of habitation on the Encounter Table ST-10-

Thi: process is called an eecounter poll and is normally performed
ome every 4 hows when a group is moving and once during each
pondd of sleep (night) when the group camps. Table 57-10 also in-
dudes modifications for the activity roll and modifications for the
avoidance roll.

Searching for adventure sites can fall into one of two different
categories: looking for aapecific site of looking for any random site

itinted because of rumonsor just a general desire for lot and adeen-
ture. Random sites could conast of animal or moniter laird, ald
tombs, abandoned tewers and temples, or deserted ruins of any
ype.

Auumiat aod Ranger in the group; and +2, for each
searcher in the group. Bonuses for growp members!
"Foraging" secondary skills may be added, but they are
first divided by the number in the group.

"Absolute Success” indicates the searchers have found
1=5 doses, “Succes” mdicates one dose was found:

and modified by +20: “Partial Success" or “Fallure™
results in nm herbs found; and “Absolute Failure® or
“Blunder” means no more doses will be fownd during the
rest of the diy.

A group may decide to split inte emaller groups for individuals),
